I've been working on something I think you'll find fascinating, and I wanted to run it by you. I want to profile Kwame Teague, a man who engineered a cultural revolution from solitary confinement and literally created an entire genre of literature that birthed shows like Power, Empire, and Godfather of Harlem. While Hollywood executives were still figuring out what "urban literature" meant, Kwame was already three moves ahead, building distribution networks through Harlem street vendors, mentoring writers who would become household names, and producing films for BET and Bravo, all while serving a life sentence. Here's what makes this story perfect for Rolling Stone: Kwame didn't just write books that sold millions of copies. He saw the music industry blueprint (how hip-hop artists controlled their own narratives and built empires) and applied it to literature and film. He turned his cell into a Hollywood production office, spending $400 a month on phone bills to participate in table reads, direct script revisions in real-time, and negotiate with Oscar winners like the late Louis Gossett Jr. This isn't a redemption story or a feel-good piece about prison reform. This is the untold origin story of how Black entertainment evolved in the 21st century. Kwame's 2004 novel Dutch was the Big Bang moment, the book that cracked open urban literature and paved the way for every street narrative that followed. But unlike most cultural pioneers, he did it while physically locked away from the very world he was transforming. What gives me unique access to tell this story? I'm also an incarcerated writer. I understand the psychological precision required to maintain creative sanity behind bars. I know what it costs (literally and figuratively) to build an entertainment career through 15-minute phone calls and handwritten letters. I've witnessed firsthand how the prison industrial complex both crushes artistic dreams and, paradoxically, sometimes forges them into something unbreakable. Kwame operates on what he calls "creative trust." He has to release his vision into the world and hope it survives the collaborative process without his physical presence. That's a creative dynamic most artists never have to navigate, and it reveals something profound about both artistic integrity and the nature of influence itself. You know Rolling Stone has always excelled at finding the cultural architects working outside traditional power structures. Kwame Teague built an empire from a cell block. His story deserves to be told by someone who understands both the creative process and the carceral system that tried to silence him. I can deliver this profile in 3,000-5,000 words, with unprecedented access and insights that only another incarcerated creative could provide. This is the intersection of mass incarceration, Black entrepreneurship, and cultural innovation, told from the inside out. Let me know if you're interested in commissioning this piece. I believe Kwame's story will resonate with Rolling Stone's readers in ways that surprise and inspire them.

backend-1  | Diamond's Literary WorldConquering Life One Book At A TimeKwame Jamal Teague aka Dutch May 31, 2015Diamondʹs Literary World Born October 21,1972 in Newark, New Jersey; Kwame Jamal Teague aka Dutch dropped out of school atage 16 and was incarcerated for life at 21. He was arrested and charged with the murder of twoindividuals near Goldsboro, NC in 1994…both of which he was found guilty. He was sentenced to (1)two life terms, (2) two fourteen year and (3) two twelve year sentences, thus…keeping him in prison forthe rest of his life. Teague still claims his innocence. As he ﬁghts for his freedom, the hip-hop ﬁctiontrilogy entitled Dutch, was penned and he became known as one of the hoĴest authors in Urban Fiction. Kwame Teague is also the ﬁrst urban writer to ghost write for rappers including C-Murder (DeathAround The Corner) and Jackie-O (Grown and Gangsta)

Kwame Jamal TEAGUE.No. COA98-1176.Decided: September 07, 1999Margaret Creasy Ciardella, Durham, for the defendant. Michael F. Easley,Attorney General, by Ronald M. Marquette, Assistant Attorney General, forthe State.In November 1995, a jury found that defendant Kwame Jamal Teague-along with Edward Lemons andLarry Leggett-kidnapped, robbed, and murdered Margaret Strickland and Bobby Stroud.   The trialcourt sentenced the defendant to two life terms for the first-degree-murder convictions, two terms offourteen years for the first-degree-kidnapping convictions, and two terms of twelve years for thearmed-robbery convictions-all sentences to run consecutively.The State's evidence at trial tended to show that on 22 January 1994 the gunshot bodies of Ms.Strickland and Mr. Stroud were found in a field located near Goldsboro, North Carolina

backend-1  | This article was first published by Nash News, a prison newspaper at Nash Correctional Institution inNashville, North Carolina. The article has been lightly edited to add clarity and conform with PJP style rules.The urban film “Dutch” premiered on Black Entertainment Television (BET) during the first week of June2021, but the current generation of viewers probably did not know that “Dutch” was originally a trilogy ofnovels written by a North Carolina prisoner 20 years ago.Kwame Teague pioneered the urban fiction genre with gritty street tales spearheaded by gangster protagonistsreminiscent of the sagas Teague witnessed as a youth. Teague was raised in a Muslim community in Newark,New Jersey, once the stolen car capital of the world. Despite strict Muslim principles, Teague recalled his neighborhood facing the same struggles as any otherinner city district: poverty, violence, crime, racial injustice and heroin addicts who could recite the Koranbackwards and forwards

backend-1  | . He has other projects in theworks: “Dynasty with Ving Rhames,” “Angel,” with MC Lyte and “Champagne” with Teanna Taylor, allscheduled for production soon.Aside from films, Teague is anxious to restart the creative writing class he taught prior to coronavirusrestrictions.One of the lessons he teaches his students is: “Tell your story, and when it resonates with the right audience, itwill be a success.” Just like “Dutch.”
backend-1  | 
backend-1  | . As a result of his upbringing, Teague witnessed how the criminal justice systemtreated marginalized citizens unfairly. After being sentenced to life in the early 90s, Teague experienced that unfairness personally. His tumultuousyouth and incarceration combined like hot and cold currents to form the perfect storm he needed to write astory that encapsulated the struggles he had faced his entire life. That story was “Dutch,” a tale about anincarcerated gangster facing an invulnerable foe: trial in an American court.Initially, Teague wanted to write “Dutch“ as a film, but he had no means of film production. He felt the storywould be popular because he knew how to communicate with his target audience, the hip-hop community.“Dutch” mirrored popular rap albums of its era with depictions of fancy cars, houses, slick-talking beautifulwomen and arrogant hustlers clawing their way to the top of the drug game. “Dutch“ was hip-hop in narrativeform
backend-1  | 
backend-1  | In “Dutch,” Teague exploited the commonality of human struggle to help upright citizens identify with a blackgangster who many would consider a stain on society. Dutch the outlaw, symbolized society’s confrontationwith the unjust powers that be. When Dutch triumphed, the people triumphed, by any means necessary. Dutchembodied the people’s revolution on paper.“Dutch” resonated with Manny Halley, an entertainment manager who handled the careers of Keisha Cole,Nicki Minaj and Young Thug. Halley wanted to break into the film industry and began talking to Teagueabout adapting “Dutch” as a screenplay. Teague said that it took about eight years to get the right people inplace, but in early 2021, “Dutch” was released in AMC theaters.Seeing his work on the silver screen did not complete Teague’s life plan, however

backend-1  | This article was first published by Nash News, a prison newspaper at Nash Correctional Institution inNashville, North Carolina. The article has been lightly edited to add clarity and conform with PJP style rules.The urban film “Dutch” premiered on Black Entertainment Television (BET) during the first week of June2021, but the current generation of viewers probably did not know that “Dutch” was originally a trilogy ofnovels written by a North Carolina prisoner 20 years ago.Kwame Teague pioneered the urban fiction genre with gritty street tales spearheaded by gangster protagonistsreminiscent of the sagas Teague witnessed as a youth. Teague was raised in a Muslim community in Newark,New Jersey, once the stolen car capital of the world. Despite strict Muslim principles, Teague recalled his neighborhood facing the same struggles as any otherinner city district: poverty, violence, crime, racial injustice and heroin addicts who could recite the Koranbackwards and forwards

backend-1  | 
backend-1  | . He has other projects in theworks: “Dynasty with Ving Rhames,” “Angel,” with MC Lyte and “Champagne” with Teanna Taylor, allscheduled for production soon.Aside from films, Teague is anxious to restart the creative writing class he taught prior to coronavirusrestrictions.One of the lessons he teaches his students is: “Tell your story, and when it resonates with the right audience, itwill be a success.” Just like “Dutch.”
backend-1  | 
backend-1  | . As a result of his upbringing, Teague witnessed how the criminal justice systemtreated marginalized citizens unfairly. After being sentenced to life in the early 90s, Teague experienced that unfairness personally. His tumultuousyouth and incarceration combined like hot and cold currents to form the perfect storm he needed to write astory that encapsulated the struggles he had faced his entire life. That story was “Dutch,” a tale about anincarcerated gangster facing an invulnerable foe: trial in an American court.Initially, Teague wanted to write “Dutch“ as a film, but he had no means of film production. He felt the storywould be popular because he knew how to communicate with his target audience, the hip-hop community.“Dutch” mirrored popular rap albums of its era with depictions of fancy cars, houses, slick-talking beautifulwomen and arrogant hustlers clawing their way to the top of the drug game. “Dutch“ was hip-hop in narrativeform
backend-1  | 
backend-1  | In “Dutch,” Teague exploited the commonality of human struggle to help upright citizens identify with a blackgangster who many would consider a stain on society. Dutch the outlaw, symbolized society’s confrontationwith the unjust powers that be. When Dutch triumphed, the people triumphed, by any means necessary. Dutchembodied the people’s revolution on paper.“Dutch” resonated with Manny Halley, an entertainment manager who handled the careers of Keisha Cole,Nicki Minaj and Young Thug. Halley wanted to break into the film industry and began talking to Teagueabout adapting “Dutch” as a screenplay. Teague said that it took about eight years to get the right people inplace, but in early 2021, “Dutch” was released in AMC theaters.Seeing his work on the silver screen did not complete Teague’s life plan, however

backend-1  | Diamond's Literary WorldConquering Life One Book At A TimeKwame Jamal Teague aka Dutch May 31, 2015Diamondʹs Literary World Born October 21,1972 in Newark, New Jersey; Kwame Jamal Teague aka Dutch dropped out of school atage 16 and was incarcerated for life at 21. He was arrested and charged with the murder of twoindividuals near Goldsboro, NC in 1994…both of which he was found guilty. He was sentenced to (1)two life terms, (2) two fourteen year and (3) two twelve year sentences, thus…keeping him in prison forthe rest of his life. Teague still claims his innocence. As he ﬁghts for his freedom, the hip-hop ﬁctiontrilogy entitled Dutch, was penned and he became known as one of the hoĴest authors in Urban Fiction.
